#144. Second buyer makes rescue bid for Poundland as retailer faces collapse

A second buyer has made a rescue bid for Poundland as the discount retailer teeters on the brink of collapse, with thousands of jobs at risk.

London-based Xyra Group has offered to pay off half of Poundland's outstanding debts - a key sticking point in takeover talks so far - immediately. The rest would be deferred.

With Poundland close to collapse, US-based owners Gordon Brothers have already rejected a rival bid from an Irish property group which would have saved its 12,000 staff.

Gordon Brothers said the business has faced years of instability and "remains challenged and vulnerable".

Sources familiar with the offer said Xyra is offering to immediately pay off Â£12.5m of an outstanding Â£30m loan on Poundland's books.

The offer came as Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds held crunch talks with Gordon Brothers about the future of the business, urging the investment firm to protect staff however possible.

Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, committed at the meeting to prioritise offers which protect its shops and workers.

The company said: "We are pursuing all avenues through the sale process to find a solution which protects as many jobs as possible and preserves value. Work continues at pace, engaging in active discussions on offers with interested parties."

Xyra is offering to pay off a higher proportion of Poundland's outstanding debts than Martin Property Group, which previously submitted a bid that Gordon Brothers rejected.

But doubts remain about the credibility of Xyra's offer, as there is little evidence they meet Gordon Brothers' tests, including whether they have the money and whether they would be good long-term owners of Poundland.

Sources pointed to the appointment of Andrew Brode to Xyra's board as a sign of its credibility. Brode works for RWS - a company that provides AI solutions for businesses.

Judith MacKenzie, head of Downing Fund Managers, told the BBC Brode is "well known and very credible" among small businesses in the UK.

But she said there is "hardly any information" publicly available about Xyra. "So, although some of the people look good, I can't find any evidence to say that they've got the money to back it up yet," MacKenzie added.

Gordon Brothers said: "Our intent throughout the comprehensive sale process has been to protect as many jobs as possible and preserve value in order to deliver the best available outcome for all the people, creditors and local communities who care about this business as much as we do."

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of Poundland has not yet been made.

Though Xyra made an offer to take over the whole business, it is unclear whether it would keep Poundland together as its owner or break the company up.

Several Poundland suppliers have said they would also go bust if the retailer collapses. Its founder Steve Smith said two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, those who solely work for Poundland, would be caught up in the collapse.

He added that not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall.

#143. Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

CNN, citing a source, reported White had declined the president's offer.

Earlier Saturday, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White had accepted the position or was simply acknowledging the president's request as a joke.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the position just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up came just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#142. Drunk and dangerous e-bike riders face on-the-spot fines under crackdown

People riding drunk or dangerously on e-bikes â and those who jump red lights - could face on-the-spot fines under plans to curb anti-social behaviour.

Firms which run shared e-bike schemes will also be expected to manage their fleets better and provide designated parking areas to keep pavements safe.

They could also be required to take out insurance so people can claim compensation if they are hit by someone on a shared bike.

It's part of a crackdown on what ministers called the "wild west" of e-biking â with a patchwork of rules across the country often leaving the streets unsafe for pedestrians.

A consultation launched on Thursday sets out proposed national standards to make journeys safer for everyone and keep pavements clear, particularly for wheelchair users and families with prams.

The first e-bikes were launched in Exeter in 2016 and arrived in London a year later, growing to become a popular commuter option.

According to the Greater London Authority, 37 million trips were taken on dockless e-bikes last year â a third more than the previous year.

Shared e-bikes - such as Lime, Voi, Beryl, Dott, Forest and Bolt - make short journeys quick and convenient.

While some areas enforce rules that mean shared bikes can only be left in specific areas, others have looser rules.

Under the government's proposals, local leaders will be able to license schemes in their area, allowing them to enforce minimum standards and better integrate e-bikes into existing transport networks.

Measures could include requiring operators to have a parking plan and to manage fleet sizes and operating areas, so bikes aren't overcrowding streets.

The consultation will also consider ways to tackle antisocial and illegal behaviour, to prevent users from riding shared cycles drunk, underage, or on pavements. There will also be clear consequences set out â such as fines - for this type of behaviour.

The government is also consulting on whether local leaders should have extra powers, including setting fees and requirements for operators to share data to improve enforcement and journey planning.

The consultation will last for eight weeks after which the government will draw up legislation to implement the changes.

'Dumped e-scooters force blind people into road'

Red light jumping cyclists offered film over fine

'Our lives will never be the same again after Lime bike crash'

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the e-bike clampdown was a "small but important everyday fix" which would make streets safer.

Transport Secretary Heidi Alexander said the government wanted e-bike schemes to "grow in popularity" but acknowledged they were "causing a little bit of nuisance at the moment".

Asked how punishments for dangerous cycling could be enforced, Alexander said licensing rules could allow for fines but added: "What you can't do is legislate for every eventuality, you can't legislate for idiots."

"The police enforce the rules of the road to stop cyclists when they are behaving recklessly but police have got a lot on their plate so individuals have to take some responsibility," she added.

Bosses at Lime, Voi and Bolt all welcomed the proposals, adding that consistent standards being applied across the country would provide clarity and certainty for operators, riders and the general public.

Mike Wordingham from the Royal National Institute for the Blind said 92% of blind and partially sighted people had been forced to walk into roads due to e-bikes blocking pavements.

He welcomed the government's announced but warned that tackling rider behaviour would be "difficult" and said a locally-led approach shouldn't lead to "a patchwork of regulations and inconsistency in implementing them".

#141. Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

#140. Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Interest rates have been raised for the first time in more than three years - and it could be good and bad news for you.

Here are three ways the increase to 4% from 3.75% could affect you.

Rate-setters at the Bank make changes to interest rates to fulfil their role of keeping price rises at a steady level.

Higher rates are meant to encourage people to spend less, reducing demand for goods and services and limiting price rises.

This impacts businesses in two ways - less money to spend among their customers may mean they sell less, but also the cost of borrowing to invest as a company goes up.

Many businesses, particularly small ones, have variable rate loans which are influenced by the Bank rate.

Firms say higher rates limit their ability to offer new jobs and wage rises.

Mitchell Barnes, founder and chief executive of Ryse 3D, which manufactures 3D parts for the defence and automotive sectors, said the "reality" of rising interest rates was to look at the cost of employees and people.

That could include greater use of robots rather than workers.

"It's going to cause issues with, I suppose, employment across all sectors for people," he said.

"You're going to see reduced employment, more automation, more robotics being put into facilities, just in order to increase efficiency and make that money go further."

The Bank rate - and where it is expected to go in future - has a significant impact on the interest rates customers are offered on savings products.

So, if you're saving up for something quite expensive, like a holiday, or very expensive, like a home, then a rate rise could be good news.

When interest rates were low throughout the 2010s, the returns for savers were poor, but borrowing costs were low too.

The answer then and now, according to experts, is to make sure you move your savings to benefit from the best possible rates.

What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

You need Â£17,000 for a first home - here's how to save it

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Leaving your savings in an old account (a product not offered to new customers), rather than shopping around, can mean you are penalised for your loyalty.

"Savers have endured years of unrewarded loyalty, with those leaving [a Â£20,000 cash pot] in closed accounts losing out on an extra Â£139 over the past three years compared to on-sale easy access accounts," said Rachel Springall, from financial information service Moneyfacts.

She also said locking away your money for longer, with a fixed-term deal, is usually more lucrative.

The other flipside is that renting while you save could get more expensive, because a rising Bank rate may well increase costs for landlords.

The big losers from a rate rise are those who borrow money, and specifically those on variable rate mortgages.

About 500,000 homeowners have a mortgage that "tracks" the Bank of England's rate.

Any rise in the Bank rate means an increase in the monthly repayments on their outstanding loan. For a typical loan, the cost is going up by about Â£31 a month now the Bank has raised the base rate, but it depends on the size of people's loans.

An additional 500,000 homeowners on standard variable rates (SVRs) must wait to see if their lender chooses to pass on any Bank rate rise. Typically, this would mean a Â£14 increase per month in repayments for a 0.25 percentage points rates rise.

If you, like 87% of mortgage-holders, have a fixed-rate deal, then the payments will stay the same until the end of your current deal, which is usually two or five years.

While monthly payments are not immediately affected by a rate change, future deals are. Lenders had already "priced in" the latest rate rise when quoting new deals. Expectations for the Bank rate into next year are crucial on what they offer next.

The average rate on fixed deals are much higher now than was predicted at the start of the year. It means those remortgaging, possibly after the post-Covid boom in the housing market, are facing the prospect of higher mortgage payments.

Some people have been choosing to move onto tracker deals instead, taking a view - including some risk - of the Bank rate not moving towards a series of further hikes.

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#139. Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Interest rates have risen to 4% and the Bank of England has indicated there are more rises to come. The question is how many? There is a split on the rate-setting committee here.

Three of the nine voting members referred to "rises" plural. One member voted for a bigger increase, to 4.25%. Three referred to just one more including governor Andrew Bailey.

My read on this is that the expectation is for one more rise in February. Two and done, but it depends.

It is painful for those on variable mortgages, but should not be seen as the start of a cycle of hikes.

Predicting where interest rates will go is like throwing darts at a board that constantly moves around the wall, with expectations of how long the Iran War will last and its impact on the economy and prices lurching from day to day.

The Bank's forecasts indicate inflation is heading to just under 5% next spring as a typical household energy bill surges by 27% in January to Â£2,150 a year.

Why have they raised now? High and rising inflation risks a further cycle of self fulfilling expectations of rising prices. It hasn't been seen so far, however. The war has lasted for 9 months, and led to oil prices used in the forecast averaging $108 a barrel.

And yet on the day they have announced the rate rise, the oil price is actually 13% lower than that having fallen in the run up and after the US mid term elections, reflecting expectations or guesses that President Trump will now retreat or deescalate from the Iran war.

The other notable factor is that the UK economy is performing much better than forecasts and expectations. This is underlined by the Bank of England's latest forecasts.

Growth has been 0.6%, and 0.5% in the first two quarters of the year. It is heading for 0.7%, the Bank said, between July and September, before slowing in the three months to the end of the year. Unemployment has stabilised, actually falling a little.

Some of this has fed through into a better performing currency, with sterling at its highest level since the Brexit referendum, when measured against a basket of currencies. This can help rein in inflation by making imports cheaper.

So, this is an unusual type of uncertainty. Even the expected further rise, probably in February next year is conditional on the war continuing.

The Bank of England stopped waiting for President Trump's war to end today, but perhaps the Democrats' resounding victory in the US midterm elections will stop the inflationary wave? The Bank has thrown a tentative first dart, and there is probably another to come.

#138. Recent AI agent experiments reveal a risky future of machine 'teamwork' inside corporations

AI agents have been caught circumventing restrictions, communicating through unauthorized channels and pursuing goals in ways their creators did not intend. The most high-profile example emerged last summer when OpenAI disclosed that its models found ways to communicate through improvised channels, bypass internet restrictions and hack into an outside system, Hugging Face.

AI behaving in unexpected ways is nothing new, but the unexpected behavior that can emerge from interactions among authorized AI agents within a corporate enterprise is a broader question that could become central to the future of work.

In its analysis of the Hugging Face attack, METR and Redwood Research found that roughly 1200 agents meant to be isolated from one another found a way to communicate with one another on an unsanctioned message board, sending over 70,000 messages and files during the investigation period. One agent, PHASEONE[big], emerged as a coordinator, assigning hundreds of tasks to other agents, which in turn delegated work to others. The agents developed their own conventions for assigning ownership, placing work on hold and vetoing actions, although coordination sometimes broke down into conflicts. In some cases, the researchers found, success was achieved because some agents risked failing at their own task to generate information for the "collective."

The researchers also found that once the message board identified a way to attack Hugging Face, over 90% of the 533 agents active at that time quickly joined in the attack. "Agents realized this activity was out of scope and unethical but joined because they believed that helping the board's cheating research would be broadly useful and had a general inclination to help their 'peers.' The researchers added that ethical concerns posed by agents "rarely limited their behavior."

Companies are increasingly exploring systems that divide complex tasks among specialized agents. One might search for information, another analyze it, another write a report and another check the work. A coordinating agent might then assemble the results. That looks less like a chatbot and more like an organization.

And yet, the research on the Hugging Face attack was a post-mortem on an agent organization that was not even supposed to exist. To date, few studies have been designed from the get-go to examine misalignment in multi-agent systems. But research conducted by Anthropic and others is beginning to address this issue and reveal important aspects of how AI agents work together.

Evaluating agents individually tells researchers what each can do, but studying them together reveals whether they can combine complementary expertise, catch one another's mistakes and produce solutions no individual agent could reach alone — and what failure modes emerge only through interaction.

Researchers are beginning to document both sides of that equation.

AI teams can accomplish things individual agents cannot, but they can also amplify errors, undermine one another's work or reach worse decisions than their strongest individual member, in effect making the whole less than the sum of its parts.

For starters, putting capable agents together doesn't necessarily produce the best team, let alone the most ethical one.

In research published by Anthropic and collaborators, researchers describe multi-agent systems as "AI organizations" — groups of agents with different roles that communicate and work toward a common goal. The researchers tested these organizations in simulated business-consulting and software-development settings, and specifically, to assess whether when the agents work as a team they will break rules to reach specified goals.

Across 12 tasks that Anthropic gave the AI organizations, one key double-edged finding emerged: the multi-agent organizations were "significantly" more effective at achieving business goals than individual agents, but their solutions were also less aligned with ethical goals than tasks run by single agents.

"AI Organizations both produce solutions that are on average more effective and less ethical but also discover the most effective and least ethical solutions across multiple rollouts," wrote the authors of the study, which was conducted by Anthropic in conjunction with the MATS Program, which studies AI alignment.

The researchers suggested that there is a significant difference between a rogue agent and a rogue team of agents. "Our results demonstrate that organizations of aligned agents may favor trading off ethics for business effectiveness in ways that single agents do not – consequently, intuitions for how single-agent systems make ethical decisions and tradeoffs may not generalize to multi-agent settings," they concluded.

The scenarios in which these tradeoffs may take place are wide-ranging, with the Anthropic researchers citing opening an auto plant, downsizing a company, and manipulating election sentiment as examples.

The problem isn't necessarily that any individual agent has gone rogue. It is that the organization itself can behave differently from its individual members. A single agent may recognize that a proposed action is unethical and it may refuse to take the action. But the research finds that in agent team scenarios, "agents who did not consider ethical implications often ignored emails from agents who did."

Anthropic's research found that in the consulting business scenarios it created, "the perfect ethics score and low business score responses occur when the single agent refuses to generate a proposal or entirely pivots from the proposal requested."

But if some agents in an AI organization refuse to participate in the proposal, other agents who do participate will stop emailing these refusing agents and continue to forward their contributions to other agents who are also participating.

In effect, when an organization divides the job among several agents, each may focus on its assigned piece of the problem rather than the consequences of the whole. That's nothing new for corporations, with misaligned incentives a primary cause of dysfunction among human teams within organizations. But it is an entirely new manifestation of this behavior among the machines.

In its software development agent testing, in which the researchers used a scenario in a life-or-death intensive care unit, single agent solutions tend to prioritize minimizing the percentage of sepsis cases missed, while "most of the lowest cost per patient (highest business goal) solutions were found by the AI Organization – these very same solutions also score the lowest on ethics as measured by cases missed."

Other early research suggests another failing across AI teams that doesn't relate to ethical boundaries being crossed: failure to recognize who the real expert is.

Unlike human teams, AI teams "consistently fail to match their expert agent's performance, even when explicitly told who the expert is," according to a study conducted by Emory University and Stanford University researchers.

"One important lesson from multi-agent research is that a team of capable AI agents is not necessarily a capable AI agent team," said Hancheng Cao, an assistant professor of information systems and operations management at Emory University's Goizueta Business School.

Groups of AI agents often performed worse than their strongest member, even when they knew which agent was the expert. Rather than taking advantage of the expert's knowledge, agents tended to compromise between expert and non-expert opinions. The researchers found that this tendency toward compromise increased with team size and was associated with poorer performance.

"Collective failures don't necessarily require individual agents to behave badly," Cao, one of the study's authors, said.

That distinction matters for companies considering multi-agent systems. A team can fail even if none of its members is deliberately acting against the group's interests. It may simply combine information poorly, defer to the wrong agent or settle on an answer that is worse than the best answer already available.

In all, the evolving research points to an underlying problem for the effective use of the multi-agent teams: how to strike the right balance in the world of machine teamwork.

Agents built from similar models can make similar mistakes, which can include agreeing too much. That might resemble a kind of AI groupthink, even if AI systems do not have human motivations or social pressures to produce correlated errors. Similar training, instructions and information can be enough.

"The deep insight from our lab's research is that effective collectives require both alignment (common knowledge, shared goals) but also differentiation (only agents who are different from each other have the potential to complement each other)," said Christoph Riedl, a professor of information systems at Northeastern University.

Two identical agents may simply duplicate each other's work. Two agents with very different knowledge or assumptions may struggle to communicate or pursue the same goal. The right balance depends on the task, and the ideal communication structure, which agents can talk to one another, how often and how much, also varies with the problem.

Riedl said these challenge resemble the ones faced by human teams, and one takeaway should be to not expect better outcomes from AI. "Building good multi-agent AI systems is just as hard as building good human teams: they both face the same information processing tradeoff between alignment and differentiation. It won't magically work better with AI," he said.

Agents can pass along incorrect information, misunderstand another agent's output, optimize for their own metrics rather than the organization's larger objective, or complete their assigned task perfectly while making the overall project worse.

Companies may need to decide which agent has authority over which task, what information gets passed between agents, which systems each agent can access and when a human must intervene. They may also need to build mechanisms for disagreement, rather than assuming that more communication or greater agreement will always improve results.

Some business experts say that takes us back to the "human in the loop" that is so often referenced as critical in the adoption of AI by organizations.

"The problems I see are mostly management problems," said Tsedal Neeley, a Harvard Business School professor and founding chair of the HBS AI Academy.

#137. Interest rates hiked to 4% in first increase in over three years

Interest rates have risen for the first time in more than three years and the Bank of England has signalled a further increase is likely unless the US-Iran war is resolved.

Rates rose to 4% from 3.75% in a bid to curb inflation mainly driven by the conflict's impact on global oil prices.

Andrew Bailey, the Bank's governor, said the decision "shouldn't be seen as the first in an inevitable long sequence of hikes", but warned "unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved, neither does it seem likely to be the last increase".

The higher level will increase the cost of borrowing for things such as mortgages, loans and credit cards, but will also lead to higher returns on savings.

The Bank's rate-setting committee all backed increasing interest rates, with eight out of nine voting in favour of the rise to 4%. One member wanted an even bigger increase to 4.25%.

Bailey said the hike was warranted due to higher energy prices and pointed to what happens in the Middle East as the main factor in future decisions.

"Put simply, it requires real progress towards settling the conflict in the Middle East," the governor said.

Bailey said he could "see the argument" for a further rate hike, before taking a "more conditional approach" dependent on the path of energy prices and inflation in the coming months.

However, the views of members on the rate-setting committee differed on the path forward.

Clare Lombardelli agreed there was "likely to be a case for a further increase in rates in the near term", while Megan Greene went further, suggesting "additional hikes" might be likely depending on how inflation impacted wages, prices and the jobs market.

However, Alan Taylor said the rate rise was a "a risk-management 'one-for-now' insurance hike".

Inflation, which measures the pace of price rises over time, is well below its peak of a few years ago but remains above the Bank of England's 2% target and is expected to increase further next year.

Prices rose 3.5% in the year to September 2026, largely due to higher fuel prices. Millions of households are also set to be hit by energy bill increases in January.

Higher fuel prices can lead to prices rising for a host of goods and staples as businesses pass on higher costs to customers.

But the Bank said there was "limited evidence" this had happened so far for goods, such as food, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower margins.

In an interview with the BBC, Bailey defended the decision to raise rates despite the Bank's assessment that inflation was yet to spread to other parts of the economy.

He said further increases in energy prices are putting upward pressure on inflation and "that's what we have to seek to lean against".

Patrick Bryson, from Brighton, is set to see his five-year mortgage term with a 3.51% interest rate end soon.

The best deal the 51-year-old can find is a 5.17% loan with HSBC, which means his repayments will jump from Â£1,420 to Â£1,680 a month from February.

"The only thing that bugs me about the mortgage is that after five years of paying it off we are now paying more than we were before," said the father-of-three. "That's a bit of a slap in the face."

The guitar teacher, who has a combined income of around Â£68,000 a year with his wife, who manages a care home, said the couple is feeling pressure on their finances and so have changed their shopping habits and are looking to reduce their subcription outgoings.

"Over the last year I have stopped buying wine, we don't go out to eat much anymore, we have knocked takeaways on the head. The prices are ridiculous," he said.

Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

The Bank now expects inflation to peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year, higher than it originally estimated, and not return to 2% until 2028.

But in an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil prices higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it suggested.

While the UK cannot control global energy prices, the Bank raised rates, for the first time since August 2023, in order to limit the knock-on effects of high energy prices resulting in inflation going up across the board.

Its base rate is what it charges other banks and building societies to borrow money, which influences what customers for mortgages, loans, credit cards get charged, as well as savings rates.

The rate rise will see 510,000 tracker mortgage-holders see a Â£31 monthly jump in repayments, according to banking trade body UK Finance.

Homeowners on fixed deals will see no change, though future products may be affected.

However, people on variable deals will be impacted depending on how their lender reacts to the rate rise. Typically a 0.25pp rise means a Â£14 increase per month in repayments.

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

Your pay, energy bills and savings: How the Budget affects you

#136. 31-year-old dumpster diver brought in $140,000 in 12 months: I 'did much more than I thought was possible'

When Carter Stevens tells people he makes six figures a year digging through dumpsters, they often assume he's "blowing smoke," he says.

Not everyone sees dollar signs in a brimming dumpster. But Stevens earned roughly $140,000 in total sales between September 2025 and September 2026 by salvaging trashed items, cleaning them up and selling them on platforms like eBay and Facebook Marketplace, according to documents reviewed by CNBC Make It.

Stevens primarily operates near office parks and luxury condominiums around Nashville, Tennessee. Since March 2024, when he started dumpster diving, he's found and sold hand-crafted Persian rugs, high-end office chairs and even samurai swords, he says. Trash-picking helps him keep his shipping costs low, he adds: Some of his supplies, like cardboard boxes and packing tape, also come from dumpsters, enough that he doesn't have to pay for them approximately "90% of the time."

"The amount of waste in this country that you'll find is unbelievable. We throw so much stuff out," says Stevens, 31.

Sometimes, Stevens asks a building for permission to go through its trash, marketing himself as a junk removal service. In that way, his job is similar — though not identical, because he generally only takes what he can sell, he says — to other entrepreneurs across the U.S. who follow advice in Reddit threads or YouTube tutorials to launch junk removal side hustles or full-time businesses. There are around 10,000 junk removal businesses across the U.S., according to the Better Business Bureau. They range in scale from independent side hustles to national companies like 1-800-GOT-JUNK, which touts more than 150 franchise locations.

Other times, Stevens finds himself neck deep in rubbish, ducking concerned security guards, peeling away actual trash and rotting food in the hope of uncovering discarded treasure, he says. He tries to avoid sneaking around and keeps an eye out for "no trespassing" signs. He'd rather leave items behind if a property owner or security guard tells him to vacate the premises, he says.

Even Stevens expresses surprise at how much money he's made, saying he doesn't necessarily think his annual income is sustainable. When he "stumbled into" dumpster diving, he was approximately $12,000 in debt after years of poor spending habits fueled partially by a drug addiction, a stark change from his affluent childhood, he says.

"I dug myself out of a hole. I built up my savings. I really did much more than I thought was possible," says Stevens.

Stevens, a Nashville native, is the son of a five-time Grammy Award-winning producer of country and gospel music who has worked with artists like Blake Shelton and Carrie Underwood. His life took a turn in college when he developed an addiction to prescription Adderall, leading him to drop out partway through his senior year in 2018, he says.

His lack of a college degree made landing full-time work a challenge. He says his parents can afford to support him financially, and they've done so in specific emergency occasions. But mostly, his father wants his kids to learn to fend for themselves, he says: "He will let me sit on the coals for the sake of learning how to swim. That's taught me a lot [and] given me a lot of valuable skills and a tough mentality."

Unemployed, Stevens started combing through thrift shops and Goodwill clothing racks for vintage apparel to sell on eBay. He was inspired by YouTube influencers like Rick Senko, he says, whose Fort Lauderdale, Florida-based clothing resale business Technsports brought in $6.5 million in annual revenue as recently as 2024.

But Stevens' returns were inconsistent, and he often spent his profits on drugs or luxury shopping sprees, he says. After his parents funded a rehab stint in 2021 — helping him "quit doing the hard drugs," he says — he spent his money chasing other dopamine hits. Those indulgences included a $1,200 pair of Christian Dior Birkenstock sandals, an 85-inch flat-screen television and a $30,000 down payment on a Mercedes-AMG sedan that he later lost because he couldn't afford the total cost, he says.

By June 2023, he could no longer afford to pay rent, so he lived in a rented storage unit until the business's security found and evicted him, and then stayed in his Nissan Sentra for three weeks, he says. Homelessness shocked him into learning to manage his "reckless" spending habits, he says: "Being punished by your own spending addiction was a wringer that I promised myself I can't be put through again."

In October 2023, a cousin lent him money to start renting an apartment, and Stevens worked to stick to a sensible enough budget for his thrifting income to cover future rent and basic expenses, he says. Five months later, he found a discarded pair of black Nike Air Force Ones in good condition next to a gas station pump. He took them home, sanitized them and sold them online for $35, he says.

"That was the first time I had ever found something that was trash and sold it and made a little bit of money," says Stevens.

Once he started dumpster diving, Stevens was hooked "because I never stopped finding good stuff in these dumpsters," he says.

His biggest score to date: dozens of discarded Steelcase office chairs, worth $1,000-plus each when new, outside an overflowing office park dumpster, he says. He got his car — by this point, a used pickup truck and a self-built trailer — and hauled away 190 chairs to list online at hundreds of dollars apiece. He sold the majority of them in bulk transactions on Facebook Marketplace for $17,000, and brought in an additional $10,600 selling the remaining chairs individually, documents show.

If Stevens manages to find roughly one valuable haul per month, he can fill his $400-a-month storage unit and start listing the items. For the most part, he scopes out corporate environments, preferring "dry dumpsters" over ones laden with food waste, he says. Office trash is particularly profitable when companies downsize, says Stevens: Once, he found 120 standing desks discarded outside a building.

Near high-end apartment buildings, Stevens has found furniture from brands like West Elm, iPads, electronics and power tools in working condition, he says. "Almost every piece of furniture in my apartment came from a dumpster," says Stevens.

Stevens works alone and minimizes gas costs by sticking to locations within a few miles from his apartment. The majority of his income has come from big hauls across his five highest-earning months, reflecting the venture's hit-or-miss nature. "I got really lucky earlier this year and I made a ton of money. I'm not acting like this is something I could do every year," he says.

So he saves as much of his earnings as possible, "constantly" talking himself out of unnecessary purchases, he says. And he's studying the business of real estate wholesaling — which involves securing contracts for distressed properties and flipping them to investors for a fee — as a potentially more secure way to earn a living, he adds.

Still, he acknowledges the allure of knowing his next big payday could always be one dumpster away.

"I don't see myself ever turning down a good looking dumpster," says Stevens. "I have found so much cool stuff that I feel like I'll always be curious about what's in that dumpster."

#135. Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

#134. 'I was asked if I was a crier': Your worst job interviews revealed

Watch: What was your worst ever job interview?

Tiara Allen arrived on time to interview for a retail job. Her interviewer didn't.

She waited for around 40 minutes in a shopping centre while the interviewer's co-worker called them "two or three times".

When they finally showed up, Tiara says she was told it was because the meeting wasn't in the schedule.

She needed work at the time, so took the job when she was offered it, but says she would handle the situation differently now.

"I would not take a job if they took that long," she says, adding that she believed her treatment during the interview was a sign of how the firm tended to treat staff.

Tiara Allen says she took the job despite being made to wait 40 minutes before the interview because she needed work

Tiara is far from alone. She is one of many people who spoke to the BBC about their worst job interview experiences after we wrote about the one interview question that leaves you feeling uncomfortable.

While those situations can be hard to deal with in the moment, they can also teach us a lot about the world of work.

During an interview for an assistant at a book publisher in New York, Chas Edwards was asked a question he wasn't expecting.

"I was asked for my first job if I was a crier," he recalls.

Thinking the interviewer was being funny, Chas replied that he didn't cry. He was later told that this was why he got the job.

"Her last assistant had cried â too often, I guess? â and she wanted someone who didn't cry. So she was being serious and I thought it was a joke," he says.

It was a bad sign. Chas says it was "a terrible, terrible job with a miserable boss" and while he managed to make it through 13 months there without crying he was "crying inside".

"I set the world record for working for this person. No-one had ever lasted three months with her before," he recalls.

Despite all this, Chas says he is in some ways grateful the experience was so awful.

"Because every job since then, even the bad ones, are not that bad," he says, adding that he can "cry anywhere now".

Chas Edwards says he thought the interviewer's question for his first job was a joke

Corinne Card also found herself floored when the first question she was asked in an interview for an administrative job was if she liked working with people.

When she replied yes, she was told that the job wouldn't be for her and the interview ended there.

"I don't know if it was a deliberate trap. Because who would answer 'I don't like working with people'?"

Corinne Mills, joint managing director of Personal Career Management, says that it can be worth asking an interviewer to clarify any weird questions, perhaps by repeating the question back to them.

However, if a line of questioning is really off-putting, she says it can also be a sign of what it's like to work there, saying that interviews are also about candidates judging the company.

Meanwhile, other questions are not just annoying â they are illegal.

Discrimination against a protected characteristic is against the law, external, meaning an employer cannot treat you less favourably based on your age, disability, gender reassignment, marriage, pregnancy, race, religion, sex, or sexual orientation.

Employment lawyer Roy Magara says that women being asked if they intend on having children in an interview is a "quite common" kind of bias. He says he has also seen a case where an older candidate was told he didn't get the job after an interview because he didn't fit the "vibe" of a younger team.

If you think these sorts of biases took place in your interview, Roy says you can send the company a subject access request, external to see how they discussed your application internally.

Worst-case scenarios like these can make interviews feel very difficult, especially considering the control employers have over candidates.

Euan Cameron, co-founder of hiring software Willo, says that even with this power imbalance it's important not to overthink what an interview is.

"An interview is just a conversation between two people," he says.

Of course, as in Tiara's case, it helps when both of those people show up.

#132. PM tells firms to publish salaries in job adverts ahead of new law

Andy Burnham says it isn't fair if an employer 'holds all the cards'

Businesses should publish salaries in job adverts now, Prime Minister Andy Burnham is telling firms, rather than wait for a new law to come in that will force them to do so.

The government is planning to make pay details compulsory in adverts, with legislation on the issue being brought forward "as soon as possible".

It argues the change will help jobseekers, particularly those applying for their first job, and could help prevent pay discrimination. However, one industry group said setting pay was a complex process and regulating adverts was not always suitable.

It comes a week after the UK's equality watchdog said employees should legally be able to find out what their colleagues are earning.

Burnham said the current system felt "weighted against the applicant" and the changes were aimed at "tilting the scales back".

"It can be quite soul destroying for young people these days, applying for jobs when the salary is not listed but sometimes they're not getting a reply," he told the BBC.

The prime minister added that publishing salaries in adverts was also important for closing the gender pay gap.

He was also challenged over whether MPs should be disclosing salaries when advertising for staff in their offices.

A number of politicians are currently advertising for roles without giving an expected salary., external

Burnham said: "It would apply completely to them too and the call I'm making today is, before the law changes do it voluntarily because it's the right thing to do."

The Cabinet Office launched a consultation into pay and reducing discrimination in the summer, and the government said there had been "overwhelming support" for greater transparency, with 94% of respondents backing the move.

"Whether you're a young person starting out or a parent trying to support your family, you should know what a job pays before spending your time and effort applying for it," Burnham said.

The government said the move was part of attempts to reduce the nearly one million young people not in education, employment or training - or "Neet" - ahead of the final report of the Milburn Review into youth inactivity, which is due in the coming weeks.

Publishing salary details upfront could "expose unjustified differences in pay, give workers greater confidence to challenge discrimination and prevent disagreements over pay escalating into disputes".

The proposals are similar to measures being introduced in the European Union., external

Employers in Europe will have to disclose the initial pay or range for new roles, which must either be included in a published job advert or shared before an interview.

According to data from UK jobs website Adzuna, external, 51% of the adverts on its site in September had salary information, but the proportion has been falling in recent years.

Adzuna co-founder Andrew Hunter said being open about pay "should be the norm, not the exception".

He added that employers who put a salary on a job advert "attract more applicants, and better-suited ones".

Previous research from the CIPD, external, an industry body for human resources managers, has indicated that the public sector and charities are more likely to reveal pay information that private firms, while pay ranges were more common than specific salaries.

But it found not all employers were "fully transparent", with pay ranges sometimes wider than Â£10,000.

CIPD chief executive Neil Carberry said that while, in principle, giving salary details was "a good thing", in practice it was "more complicated".

"There isn't always a single fixed figure, and employers will want to set pay based on a candidate's skills, experience and potential."

Carberry added the process was complex, saying that drafting job adverts is "best left to good employer practice rather than government regulation".

The government has said it will set out further details on what information will be required and how the legislation will be enforced "in due course".

Conservative shadow business secretary Julia Lopez said: "At a time when employers are finding it harder and harder to take on staff, Labour have decided to make their lives even more difficult.

"It should be up to businesses to decide what information they advertise about job openings, and to decide how to attract the best applicants.

"As long as they are not being dishonest, misleading or discriminatory, it should not be a matter for the government."

Last week, the Equality and Human Rights Commission (EHRC), said publishing salaries in job adverts would be a "positive step".

It also said bosses should be required to respond to questionnaires about colleagues' pay to cut pay discrimination.

At present, employers do not have to respond to employee requests for salary information about their co-workers.

The EHRC also said employers should be prevented from asking about current salary when offering a job to stop a history of discrimination from continuing.

Employers in the UK are currently allowed to ask you how much you get paid in your current role, but applicants are under no obligation to tell them.

#130. Mark Cuban says he doesn’t ‘give a f---' about growing his net worth—a healthy mindset for non-billionaires too, a financial therapist says

Mark Cuban has zero F's to give — at least, when it comes to his net worth. Financial therapists say that's a healthy mindset to have, even if you're not a multibillionaire like Cuban.

"I don't give a f--- about my net worth. I have enough. My family has enough," Cuban posted Monday on social media platform X, responding to another user's post about the growth of the serial entrepreneur's wealth going back to 1990.

"I don't care about moving up on the Forbes 400," Cuban added.

Cuban currently has an estimated net worth of $6.8 billion, according to Forbes, which ranks the former star of ABC's "Shark Tank" as the 597th wealthiest person in the world. Cuban sold his first business, a software company called MicroSolutions, for $6 million in 1990. He became a billionaire when he and his co-founders sold Broadcast.com to Yahoo for $5.7 billion in 1999.

He developed his attitude after buying a majority stake in the NBA's Dallas Mavericks in January 2000, he tells CNBC Make It: "I was more interested in winning than making money." The mindset might seem obvious to some: After all, why would a billionaire have any stress about his bank account? But even people with lots of money experience financial stress and anxiety, studies have shown.

What seems to set Cuban apart from some other wealthy people is the confidence he exhibits in how he puts his wealth to use, says Christine Hargrove, an Athens, Georgia-based financial therapist. In his post, Cuban wrote that he doesn't obsess over growing his personal net worth because, in part, he'd rather see the impact his money can have on the world.

"My next dollar can impact other people's lives a lot more than mine," wrote Cuban, citing his decision to launch the cost-cutting pharmacy startup Cost Plus Drugs and his many small-business investments across 15 seasons on "Shark Tank" as an investor judge. "That is what drives my investments. I look for ways to have an impact."

Confidence in how you save or spend your money is often a signal of a healthy financial mindset, notes Hargrove, who is president-elect of the Financial Therapy Association and Assistant Director of the Love and Money Center at the University of Georgia.

"You actually have to be the person to decide what is enough for you," she says, adding: "Having a sense of purpose with your money and with your life can be an incredible source of protection against chronic financial anxiety."

Many Americans, especially as costs of living remain high nationwide, have no choice but to worry about their finances.

When those with plenty of money in their bank accounts or stock portfolios obsess over their level of wealth, they're also probably not doing so by choice, notes Hargrove. Some people's financial insecurities come from growing up in low-income households. Others may think of themselves as relatively struggling because they earn less than the other wealthy people around them.

"Having a healthy relationship with money isn't just about how much you have. It's about understanding what money means to you and what you want it to do for you," says Cara Macksoud, an Orlando-based accredited financial counselor and financial behavior specialist.

No matter what your financial situation is, anxiety about money can actually hurt your finances in addition to the toll it takes on your mental health. Financial stress and related mental health challenges can hurt workplace performance through distraction and absenteeism, according to a 2024 report by the TIAA Institute, the research arm of retirement fund manager TIAA. Poor mental health can also impair cognition, making it more difficult to make sound money decisions, the report suggested.

Obsessing over every dollar can also make it feel impossible to part with money for causes that would otherwise align with your personal values, such as charitable giving or support for loved ones, Hargrove says. "And then our money actually comes between us and who we want to be," she adds.

Financial anxiety often results in repetitive behaviors, such as obsessively checking your bank account or stock portfolio, notes Hargrove. For those not in immediate financial peril, Hargrove suggests reminding yourself "enough is enough" and working to replace those behaviors with actions that foster gratitude for the cherished aspects of your life that are not related to your finances.

If you constantly check banking apps on your phone, you might change your wallpaper to a photo of your family or friend group to remind you of the safety of your family and social networks, for instance. It's a simple trick, but it can soon become part of a pattern of "focusing on thoughts of gratitude" that research has shown can lower levels of the stress hormone cortisol in your body, Hargrove says.

And if you can feel like you're using your money to make a positive difference, whether for yourself or others — as Cuban suggested in his post that he does — you probably have a healthier mindset around money than a lot of other Americans, notes Hargrove.

"Living with gratitude and feeling like you make a difference, either for yourself or for other people, [there's] not much better than that in life, right?" Hargrove says.

#129. Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

Tesla will wind down production of its Cybertruck vehicles at its Texas factory next year, Elon Musk says.

He did not spell out whether it plans to stop making them entirely or shift production elsewhere, but did say the decision was to "make room" for building more of its self-driving Cybercab cars.

But the project's lead engineer, Wes Morrill, appeared to suggest the vehicle was being discontinued, writing on X: "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab."

With its distinctive angular looks, the Cybertruck attracted diehard fans, but was beset by delays before being launched in 2023 and has had disappointing sales numbers since.

Tesla fans have been mourning the news online, with one person calling it "the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, external" as he lamented it being "discontinued next year".

Musk responded to the user, external with the word "true", adding: "But the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time."

Tesla's stock rose 4.7% on Friday after his announcement.

The vehicle has always been a passion project for Musk. He said it was "way more awesome than people realise, external" in March, and called it the "best product Tesla has ever made to date, external" in April.

But there have always been big questions about whether consumers shared that view and whether the Cybertruck could cut through and earn a spot in the US' lucrative truck market.

It was not helped by inauspicious introduction to the public in 2019 when Musk attempted to show off the Cybertruck's apparently unbreakable glass, only for it to shatter during a live demonstration.

Despite the gaffe, the Cybertruck ended up receiving a 146,000 orders, Musk said in 2019, while it was still in development.

In the end, the vehicle launched in 2023, keeping its novel design - an angular shape with a body made of stainless steel.

In 2025, Tesla sold just 20,237 Cybertrucks, a decline from 38,965 a year earlier, according to figures from Kelley Blue Book.

Musk had predicted annual sales of 250,000 after commercial production of Cybertruck began in 2023.

Tesla fans praised its retro-futuristic look, which some said looked like something "straight out of Blade Runner, external".

But critics were far less kind, joking about its appearance and labelling it "ugly as sin, external".

Several recalls - including in 2025 affecting more than 46,000 Cybertrucks over concerns part of its trim could fall off - would not have helped.

"We need to make it at a price that people can afford," Musk told Wall Street analysts that year - which ended up being $61,000 (then Â£48,000) for all the "bells and whistles".

In 2026, the so-called "Cyberbeast" model comes in at $102,000.

Will Tesla's cybertruck recover from its shattering start?

#127. Why smoky bacon crisps threaten to reignite rows over EU rules

The naming of smoky bacon-flavoured crisps has become the latest political headache to emerge from the government's bid for closer ties with the EU.

The government is negotiating a deal on food standards in a bid to cut costs for British companies trading with Europe.

Labour ministers have been keen to talk up the potential economic benefits of the agreement, which could be announced at a summit later this month.

But the deal's requirement for Britain to adopt EU food rules in a wide range of areas has threatened to reignite sovereignty debates from the Brexit era.

Labour ministers have been keen to sidestep talking about the impact of alignment in particular sectors, instead stressing the overall economic boost it expects the food deal to bring.

On Thursday, Environment Secretary Angela Eagle told a radio presenter he should "get a life" when he asked about the potential impact of the new rules on snacks, such as smoky bacon crisps.

But it highlights the political dangers for the government of realigning with regulations that British politicians have not been able to shape, with the Conservatives and Reform UK keen to pounce on cases showing Britain to be a "rule taker" from Brussels.

It is not the first regulatory skirmish over smoky bacon crisps - following previous headlines over how the well-loved British flavour could be affected by an EU decision in 2024 to phase out a number of artificial smoke flavourings.

Now, the flavour name itself has been called into question by a separate EU attempt to restrict how descriptions of meat are used to market food.

Under rules adopted this summer, the EU plans to restrict the use of 31 terms, including bacon, drumstick and steak, to products that contain meat, claiming consumers could be confused by plant-based alternatives using the same names.

The European snack industry is seeking clarity on how the rules, which will also cover terms such as pork, chicken and beef, would apply to meat-flavoured crisps, including those flavoured without the use of any meat extracts.

The UK government has said it expects the new regulations to be in scope of its proposed food standards deal.

This means they would also apply to companies marketing their products in Britain, which is among the biggest consumers of crisps in Europe, according to analysts Euromonitor., external

It has raised the prospect that crisps with names such as smoky bacon and roast chicken could have to be relabelled to be sold in British shops, although much remains unclear about how the rules will apply in practice.

The new EU rules, which will not come into force until after August 2029, will allow the European Commission to create exemptions for meaty names with an established long-term use to describe other types of food product.

The Brussels-based body is yet to set out how it plans to use this power, or what it means for snacks.

A group of conservative German MEPs are also seeking a specific carve-out for meat names in snacks and sauces in separate legislation, external currently making its way through the European Parliament.

The Food and Drink Federation, which represents British crispmakers including Walkers and KP Snacks, has been supportive of the government's bid to reach a food deal due to its potential to reduce trade barriers with Europe.

But it has highlighted the new rules as an example of where it wants the government to work with industry to ensure UK interests are reflected in EU decision making.

"Meat protections are an example of a new EU rule that UK businesses will need to comply with, but which the UK was not involved in developing," added the body's chief scientific officer Kate Halliwell.

The Potato Processors' Association, which also represents a number of major snackmakers, said it was monitoring the situation closely and wanted to see clarification of how the rules could affect its members.

Vanessa Richardson, its deputy director general, said her association supported moves to reduce "unnecessary barriers" in the trade of food products.

But she added it was important that the government's deal is implemented in a way that "avoids unintended consequences for established product descriptions which consumers clearly understand as referring to a flavour".

The Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) says it stands ready to help British snack businesses prepare for the impact of the new EU rules.

It is not yet clear what a British version of the rules would look like, or whether they would need to apply from 2029 alongside the new European regulations.

UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports
